Tokenisation over Bounded Alphabets is Hard
Violeta Kastreva, Philip Whittington, ​​ Dennis Komm, ​ Tiago Pimentel

Abstract
Recent works have shown that tokenisation is 𝖭𝖯 \mathsf{NP} -complete. However, these works assume tokenisation is applied to inputs with unboundedly large alphabets—an unrealistic assumption, given that in practice tokenisers operate over fixed-size alphabets, such as bytes or Unicode characters. We close this gap by analysing tokenisation over bounded n {\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n} -ary alphabets, considering two natural variants: bottom-up tokenisation and direct tokenisation , where we must, respectively, select a sequence of merge operations or a vocabulary whose application optimally compresses a dataset. First, we note that proving hardness results for an n {\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n} -ary alphabet proves the same results for alphabets of any larger size. We then prove that even with binary alphabets, both variants are not only 𝖭𝖯 \mathsf{NP} -complete, but admit no polynomial-time approximation scheme (unless 𝖯 = 𝖭𝖯 \mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP} ). We further show that direct tokenisation remains 𝖭𝖯 \mathsf{NP} -complete even when applied to unary alphabets. While unary alphabets may not be practically useful, this result establishes that the computational intractability of tokenisation is not an artifact of large alphabets or complex constructions, but a fundamental barrier. Overall, our results explain why practical algorithms such as BPE and UnigramLM are heuristic, and points toward approximation algorithms being an important path going forward for tokenisation research.



1
Introduction

Tokenisation
is the first step in most natural language processing pipelines.
Given a string of characters
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}
, a tokeniser maps it to a sequence of subwords
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}
.
Language models then operate on these subword sequences rather than the raw characters.
Despite its central role, however, we still lack a comprehensive understanding of tokenisation;
e.g., which properties of the produced strings of subwords
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}

actually help downstream modelling?
A common property to aim for is
compression

(
16
;
18
;
21
)
, as using shorter subword-strings to encode a dataset allows for more efficient training and inference—more data can be passed through the model with the same number of flops.
While not a silver bullet
(
15
;
1
)
, compression has been shown to correlate with downstream model performance
(
5
;
14
;
20
;
7
)
and will be our work’s focus.

A practical concern follows immediately: once an objective (e.g., compression) is fixed, can an optimal tokeniser be found efficiently?
Popular algorithms such as BPE and UnigramLM are greedy or heuristic and need not return an optimal tokeniser
for the metrics they are designed to optimise.
Further, recent work has sharpened this picture, proving the
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness of finding an optimal tokeniser under a compression-style objective
(
10
;
19
;
12
)
.
These papers, however, show this for the tokenisation of strings over unboundedly large alphabets.
Conversely, in practice the strings we care about are typically composed of Unicode characters or bytes, thus using bounded alphabets.
Whether it is possible to efficiently find optimal tokenisers over Unicode-strings (which have an alphabet size of roughly
170{,}000
), byte-strings (with an alphabet size of 256), or bit-strings (with an alphabet size of 2) are open questions of practical relevance.

In this paper, we first define the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problem
: the
problem of finding an optimal tokeniser on strings constrained to alphabets of size
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
.
We examine this problem under two variants: direct and bottom-up tokenisation, where—given a dataset over an
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary alphabet and a vocabulary size
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}
—we must find the vocabulary (in direct tokenisation) or sequence of merges (in bottom-up tokenisation) which, when applied to the dataset, maximally compresses it.
We prove that (i) assuming
\mathsf{P}\neq\mathsf{NP}
, both direct and binary bottom-up tokenisation are not in the
polynomial-time approximation scheme
(
\mathsf{PTAS}
) class, meaning that they cannot be approximated arbitrarily well in polynomial time,
1
1

1

More specifically, we present a constant lower bound on the approximation ratio achievable by any polynomial-time algorithm for this problem (again, assuming
\mathsf{P}\neq\mathsf{NP}
).

and that (ii) for the direct case, even unary tokenisation is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
Notably, unary and binary are the easiest of the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problems, and thus these hardness results also trivially extend to tokenisation problems with larger alphabets.

Our results thus indicate that the computational hardness of tokenisation is not an artifact of large alphabets or elaborate merge operations: it already appears under direct tokenisation over unary alphabets.
This helps explain why practical algorithms (e.g., BPE) rely on approximations, and suggests that future work should focus on provably good approximate methods or on relaxations for this problem.

2
Tokenisation

​​Our notation’s colour-coding (following
19
)

•

Blue
for raw data (i.e., characters
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
);

•

Magenta
for tokeniser-specific data (i.e., subwords
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}
and merges
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}
);

•

Orange
for functions (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}
).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
be
2
2

2

We note that
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
denotes the Kleene star of
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}
(i.e.,
\cup_{i=0}^{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{i}
), and
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}
denotes its Kleene plus (i.e.,
\cup_{i=1}^{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{i}
).
a
character-string
, composed of characters
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}
from an alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}
; for notational convenience, we may write one such string as
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}_{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}_{2}\dots{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}_{|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}|}}
.
Character-strings compose the raw text data found, say, on the web, which make up the datasets on which language models are trained.
We denote one such dataset by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M}
.
Before feeding data to our models, however, we typically convert them to strings of subwords, which is the job of a tokeniser.

Formally, a tokeniser can be defined as a tuple
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}},{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{detok}},{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\rangle
, composed of a vocabulary, a decoding and an encoding function.
A
vocabulary

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}
is a finite set of subwords, each of which is a non-empty span of characters; we thus write
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}
.
A
subword-string
is then a sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}
and represents a character-string via the concatenation of its subwords’ characters.
We say that a pair of character- and subword-strings are equivalent if

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\,\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\circ}}}{{=}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}\iff{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}={\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{concat}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}),\hskip 22.99988pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{concat}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}})={\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}\circ\dots\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}|}}

(1)

where
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}={\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}},\cdots,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}|}}\rangle}
,
each
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}t}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}

is a subword, and operator
\circ
denotes string concatenation.
Notably,
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}\!\subseteq\!{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}
is typically enforced to guarantee that every
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
can be represented by at least one subword-string
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}
,
and
we say that a vocabulary’s size is
|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}|+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}
.
Second in the tuple above, a
decoding function
is defined as
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{detok}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
,
and given a subword-string it outputs the character-string it represents.
This function thus is simply defined as
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{detok}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}})\,\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{concat}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}})
.

Finally, an
encoding function

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}
maps character- to subword-strings while ensuring the equivalence
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\,\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\circ}}}{{=}}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}\mathop{=}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})
.
Several encoding functions may respect this constraint, as many subword-strings may be equivalent to a specific character-string.
For instance, given

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\!=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}a},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}aa},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}aaa}\}
, the string
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\!=\!{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}aaa}
could be tokenised as
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}={\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle aaa\rangle}
or as
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}={\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle a,aa\rangle}
.
We focus on two encoding functions in this paper, which we follow
19
in labelling as direct and bottom-up.
The
direct encoding function
(
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}
) only requires a vocabulary, which it applies optimally to encode a character-string.
In turn, the
bottom-up encoding function
(
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\uparrow}}
) takes a merge sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}}\rangle
as input, which it applies in order to a character-string;
each of these merges
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}_{k}
is composed of a pair of subwords, which we represent as
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[1]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\circledcirc}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[2]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}}
,
and we write
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{merge}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}
to represent a function which, given a subword-string, processes it left-to-right and replaces any consecutive occurrence of the pair
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[1]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[2]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}
with a new token
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{[new]}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}={\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[1]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[2]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k}}
.
Defining
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\times{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}
, we say
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}
.
We now formalise these encoding functions as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\argmin_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}}|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}|,\hskip 22.99988pt\hskip 22.99988pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\uparrow}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}]({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}\smash{\bigg(\bigodot_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{merge}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}}\bigg)}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})

(2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.}\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\circ}}}{{=}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}

where
\bigodot
represents function composition.
A tokeniser is thus fully determined by a vocabulary or merge-sequence; for the direct case we have
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]
, while for bottom-up
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\uparrow}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}]
.
Importantly, the direct encoding function (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]
) can be efficiently computed in
O(|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}|^{2})
time using the methods from
15
.

2.1
Objective Functions and their Optimisation

As described above, a direct tokeniser is fully determined by a vocabulary, while a bottom-up tokeniser is identified by a merge-sequence.
How to select a specific tokeniser, though?
This is typically done via defining an objective function
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}
which, given an encoding function (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}
) and a dataset (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}
), returns a value representing the cost of that particular choice.
Choosing a tokeniser then “simply” requires optimising this objective: e.g., for direct tokenisation we must find
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}=\argmin_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})
under the constraint that
|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}|+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}
.

Several objective functions exist.
UnigramLM
(
11
)
, for instance, selects a vocabulary which optimises a dataset’s unigram negative log-probability.
Other work has proposed alternative measures, such as the frequency of the 5-th % least frequent token
(
8
)
, or the tokeniser’s Rényi efficiency
(
20
)
.
As mentioned above, we focus on compression in this paper.
We do so following a battery of previous work which formally analyses tokenisers
(
21
;
10
;
19
;
12
)
.
Prior work has shown that a tokeniser’s compression correlates with the downstream performance of language models trained on its output subword-strings
(
5
;
20
)
.
We note, however, that other recent work has criticised compression as the sole objective for tokenisation, showing that these two properties (compression and downstream performance) may have a more complex relationship than originally suspected
(
1
;
15
)
.

There are two natural ways to define a compression objective:

compressed length
, which measures the number of remaining symbols after a string is tokenised,
and
compression reduction
, which measures how many symbols are reduced in the string by a tokeniser. These are formalised as:

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}\sum\nolimits_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}}|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})|}_{\!\!\!\!\text{{compressed length}, size of remaining string}\!\!\!\!\!\!},\hskip 22.99988pt\hskip 11.49994pt\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\texttt{r}}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}\sum\nolimits_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}}\Big(|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})|-|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}|\Big)}_{\!\!\!\!\text{{compression reduction}, number of reduced symbols}\!\!\!\!}

(3)

While equivalent in how they rank tokenisers, this choice can make a big difference when evaluating the quality of an approximation.
When using minimisation objectives, such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}
, the
approximation ratio
of an algorithm upper-bounds the ratio between the objective value achieved by the algorithm’s solution and an optimal solution, being thus at least
1
by definition.
A similar definition applies when using maximisation objectives, such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\texttt{r}}}
, but the approximation ratio is inversed.
We say we have a
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-approximation algorithm
if, for every possible input, this ratio is bounded from above by
\delta
.
If a dataset has
1{,}000
characters and would have
100
symbols if optimally compressed, a suboptimal tokeniser which instead reduces it to at most
200
symbols would have an approximation ratio of
2
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}
but of
1.125
under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\texttt{r}}}
.
Notably, prior work has analysed both these measures.
We argue here that compressed length is the more natural objective, as it directly relates to the throughput achieved by a language model processing that text, being thus connected to the model’s training and inference costs.
A 2-approximation for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}
implies that a language model using that tokeniser may be 2-times slower (and more costly) than optimal when processing the same text.
3
3

3

Assuming that language models cannot achieve sub-linear computational complexity on their input’s length.

After deciding on an objective function, such as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}
above, we must select a vocabulary (
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}
) or merge-sequence (
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}
) which optimises it.
Unfortunately, both these optimisation problems have infinite search spaces (respectively,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{P}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{P}}
denotes the powerset operation), which begs the question: is there an efficient way to find these optima?
Recent work has shown that, in general, this is not possible, proving compression-based tokenisation to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete;
more specifically,
10
showed this for bottom-up tokenisation,
19
for direct and bottom-up tokenisation, and
12
for direct tokenisation with candidate tokens.
This means that, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
, there exists no polynomial-time algorithm to find compression-optimal tokenisers.
Beyond that, using the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\texttt{r}}}
objective function,
10
showed that bottom-up tokenisation is not only
\mathsf{NP}
-complete but also APX-hard, which implies that it is not in the polynomial-time approximation scheme (
\mathsf{PTAS}
) complexity class (unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
).
The
\mathsf{PTAS}
class is characterised by problems for which:
for every constant
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a polynomial-time algorithm (whose run-time may depend on
\varepsilon
), which solves it with an approximation ratio upper-bounded by
1+\varepsilon
.
Not being in
\mathsf{PTAS}
thus implies that there is no polynomial-time algorithm which can approximate the optimal solution with an approximation ratio arbitrarily close to
1
.
Notably, all of these complexity proofs apply to tokenisation problems over alphabets of arbitrarily large sizes.
Whether these results hold once alphabet sizes are bounded by a constant is thus left open.

3
Tokenisation over Bounded Alphabets

We now move to the analysis of tokenisation over bounded alphabets.
Let an
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}
-ary alphabet
be an alphabet with size
|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}|={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
.
We define the tokenisation problem over such bounded alphabets as follows.

Definition 1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}
be a vocabulary size and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}
be a dataset composed of character-strings from an alphabet of size
|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}|={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
.
For a given
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
, the
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}
-ary tokenisation decision problem
requires deciding whether there exists a vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}
(for direct tokenisation) or a merge-sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}
(for bottom-up tokenisation) which compresses
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}
to at most
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
symbols.
The
\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}
-ary tokenisation optimisation problem
is to find what the maximal such compression of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}
is. Formally:

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\geq\!\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum\nolimits_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}}\left|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\right|,\,\mathrm{s.t.}\,\,|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}|={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}}_{{\text{${\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}$-ary tokenisation decision problem}}},\,\,\,

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta_{\mathtt{opt}}}=\!\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum\nolimits_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}}\left|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\right|,\,\mathrm{s.t.}\,\,|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}|={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}}_{{\text{${\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}$-ary tokenisation optimisation problem}}}

(4)

where

\mathcal{T}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]\!\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\!\subset\!{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}\}
for direct tokenisation and
\mathcal{T}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\uparrow}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}]\!\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}\!\in\!{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}\}
for bottom-up.

We will more specifically call these the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary direct tokenisation problem and the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary bottom-up tokenisation problem when dealing with, respectively, direct and bottom-up tokenisers, writing
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\uparrow}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
for the functions which return the solution to their decision problems.
Notably, the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problems form a clear hierarchy from easiest (
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}=1
) to hardest (
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}\rightarrow\infty
), with unary tokenisation being the easiest such problem.
In the next sections, we first prove that both binary direct and binary bottom-up tokenisation are hard to approximate, i.e., that both these problems are not in
\mathsf{PTAS}
(in
section

4
).
We then prove that unary direct tokenisation is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete (in
section

5
).

Fact 1
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, all
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}^{\prime}
-ary tokenisation problems for
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}^{\prime}>{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
are
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Proof.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n},{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}^{\prime}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
.
Any instance of the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problem is a valid instance of the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}^{\prime}
-ary problem with the same solutions, allowing for a trivial reduction between them.
Thus, any proof of hardness for the
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problem immediately applies to
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}^{\prime}
-ary problems.
∎

A Note on Optimisation vs. Decision Problems.

Typically,
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness is discussed mainly as a property of decision problems,
while hardness of approximation (and consequently, being contained or not in
\mathsf{PTAS}
) is a notion regarding optimisation problems.
There is, however, a notion of equivalence between these classes of problems: if a polynomial-time algorithm exists to solve a decision problem (i.e., if this problem is not
\mathsf{NP}
-hard), it can usually be leveraged to also find an efficient algorithm for its associated optimisation problem, and
vice-versa
.
Similarly, if no polynomial-time algorithm can solve an optimisation problem with an approximation ratio arbitrarily close to 1 (i.e., if the problem is not in
\mathsf{PTAS}
), this implies that there must be some constant
\varepsilon
such that it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to distinguish between instances that admit a solution with cost
x
and those that admit a solution with cost
(1+\varepsilon)x
.
We will use this latter property here to show hardness of approximation, relying on gap-preserving reductions.
4
4

4

We note that hardness of approximation is not formally the same as proving APX-hardness
(
10
, as was done in)
. However, it allows for the same conclusion:
the binary (and larger) tokenisation problems cannot be approximated arbitrarily well in polynomial time, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.
Additionally, our gap-preserving reductions allow us to find explicit constants to which the problems cannot be approximated.

To this end, it will be useful to also define gap versions of the problems we discuss.
Formally, we will denote such gap versions
similarly to their decision versions (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
above), but while providing two decision boundaries instead (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{-},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{+}))
).
In minimisation gap problems, the task is then to decide whether their optimal value is at most
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{+}
or at least
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{-}
(with the opposite being true for maximisation problems); if a value falls between these, any answer is acceptable.
For
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problems, for instance, we would require an algorithm which computes:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{-},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{+}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}&\texttt{if }\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{+}\geq\!\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}}\left|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\right|,\,\,\mathrm{s.t.}\,\,|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}|={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}}&\texttt{elif }\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{-}\leq\!\min_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}\in\mathcal{T}}\sum_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}}\left|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\right|,\,\,\mathrm{s.t.}\,\,|{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}|={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}\\
?&\texttt{else}\end{array}\right.

4
Binary Tokenisation is Hard to Decide and Approximate

In this section, we will prove
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of the two binary tokenisation decision problems above, and of their corresponding gap problems (for specific gaps).
To this end, we will use a reduction from the
3-occurrence maximum 2-satisfiability
problem (
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
), which we define in
section

4.1
.
We then move on to proving results showing hardness of approximation for the binary direct and binary bottom-up tokenisation problems (in
section

4.2
and
section

4.3
, respectively).

4.1
3-Occurrence Maximum 2-Satisfiability

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}
be a Boolean variable assigned a value
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\}
, and let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}
be a set of such variables, with joint assignment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}
.
Further, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}=\{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2})\}_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}}
be a set of clauses,
5
5

5

In some formalisations,
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
allows clauses of size one. We work here, more specifically, with the 3-occurrence maximum exact-2-satisfiability variant of this problem, thus not allowing single literal clauses.

where each literal
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}
is either a variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}
or its negation
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}
.
We define
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
as follows.

Definition 2
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}
be a set of Boolean variables and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}=\{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2})\}_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}}
be a set of clauses.
Further, let each variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}
occur in exactly three clauses.
Given a target
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}
, the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
decision problem
requires deciding whether there exists an assignment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}
such that at least
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
clauses are satisfied.
The
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
optimisation problem
requires finding the maximum number of satisfiable clauses. Formally:

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\leq\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}}\smash{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbbm{1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}\}}_{\text{{3-OCC-MAX2SAT} decision problem}}\hskip 22.99988pt\hskip 22.99988pt\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}_{\texttt{opt}}}=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}}\smash{\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbbm{1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}\}}_{\text{{3-OCC-MAX2SAT} optimisation problem}},

(8)

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
to denote a function which, given an instance of the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
decision problem, returns its solution.
The
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
problem was proven to be hard to approximate arbitrarily well by
3
, with their result also implying that this problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

4.2
Binary Direct Tokenisation is Hard to Decide and Approximate

In this section, we prove that the binary direct tokenisation problem is both hard to decide and to approximate beyond a certain constant
r>1
.
First, we will prove that the
decision
version is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard (in
section

4.2.1
).
Second, we will then use this initial result to prove that a
gap
version of the problem is similarly
\mathsf{NP}
-hard (in
section

4.2.2
).
This will complete our proof that this problem’s
optimisation
version is hard to approximate arbitrarily well, as being contained in
\mathsf{PTAS}
would allow us to solve the gap problem.

4.2.1
The Binary Direct Tokenisation Decision Problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-Hard

We now prove
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness of binary direct tokenisation, which
requires two things: inclusion in
\mathsf{NP}
and being
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.
Inclusion in
\mathsf{NP}
follows from the general (unbounded) case, which was previously proven by
19
.
Proving
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness requires a polynomial-time reduction from another
\mathsf{NP}
-hard problem to this problem, which we will design in what follows.

Reduction 1
.

Consider an instance of the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
decision problem and the binary alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0},{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}\}
.
Now, for each variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}
, let
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{2j-1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{2j}
, i.e., these are character-strings formed of
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}
repeated
2j-1
or
2j
times.
Then we build subdatasets:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1},\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}\mid 1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\}\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1},\,\,{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}\mid 1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\}\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}

(9a)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}\mid 1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}=\!\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}\mid 1\leq i\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}\}

\displaystyle\times 1

(9b)

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}
are replaced by their respective variable characters as they appear in the
i
-th clause (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}
is replaced by
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}
if it is equal to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}
or by
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}
if it equals
\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j}
).
Further,
\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}
denotes that a set of strings should be repeated
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}
times in the corresponding dataset.
These multiplicities are
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}7
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}2({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3)+1=21
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}2({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3)+1=63
.
A full dataset is then formed by joining these subdatasets:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
.
Finally, we set the number of allowed tokens to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}=5{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
and the target compression to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}=4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}=329{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
.

6
6

6

This reduction is inspired by
19
’s
19
reduction, which we update to (i) rely on binary, as opposed to unbounded, alphabets; (ii) use constant-sized
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}
’s, which allow us to prove approximation hardness.

We will write
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
to represent the
D-2-TOK
instance which is output by this reduction, represented by the tuple
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
.
Notably, this reduction runs in polynomial time.
By proving its correctness, thus, we can show that binary direct tokenisation is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.
For this reduction to be correct, the given
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance must be satisfiable if and only if its reduced tokenisation instance is as well, i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})\iff{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
.
We now set out to prove both directions of this iff clause.

Theorem 1
.

The binary direct tokenisation decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

Proof sketch.

This proof is done in two steps.

Forward step (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
).

See a formal proof in
lemma

1
in
appendix

A
.
Assuming an instance of
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
is satisfied by assignment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}
, we build a direct tokeniser with tokens
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
, and with token
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
otherwise.
This tokeniser compresses
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
to
252{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
to
63{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
to
14{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
to
3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}
tokens, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}
is the number of clauses satisfied by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
.
Adding these compressed lengths together, we find that they satisfy the direct tokenisation problem, as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
by assumption.

Backward step (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
).

See full proof in
lemma

2
in
appendix

B
.
We first show that an optimal tokeniser for the
D-2-TOK
instance
is always
sat
-compliant
: it contains all tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
, and either
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\}
.
We do this by showing that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
guarantees that any optimal solution includes tokens
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
guarantees that any optimal solution
further only includes tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
; and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
guarantees that either token
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
exist for each
j\in\{1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\}
.
Then, we show that if such a
sat
-compliant tokeniser reaches the desired compression, it must correspond to an assignment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
which satisfies the desired number of clauses.∎

4.2.2
The Binary Direct Tokenisation Gap Problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard

We now prove that not only the decision version of the binary direct tokenisation problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, but so is its gap version.
Proving
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of a gap problem is an indirect way of proving that its optimisation version is hard to approximate: if an efficient algorithm can approximate the optimisation problem arbitrarily well (which is thus contained in
\mathsf{PTAS}
), it could be used to solve the gap problem.

Theorem 2
.

The binary direct tokenisation gap problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.
Thus, the binary direct tokenisation optimisation problem is not in
\mathsf{PTAS}
, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.

Proof sketch.

See a formal proof in
appendix

C
.
As shown by
2
;
3
, the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
gap problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to approximate for problems with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}=2016n
clauses,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{-}=(2011+\varepsilon)n
lower bound, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{+}=(2012-\varepsilon)n
upper bound.
We can use
lemmas

1
and
2
to prove a reduction from this gap problem to
D-2-TOK
’s gap problem.
Notably, our reduction equates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}=329{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
, for both
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{-}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}^{+}
.
Analysing the gap of the resulting tokenisation problem, we find that this problem is thus
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for an approximation ratio
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{-}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}^{+}}
of at least
1.000002
.
This implies that no polynomial-time algorithm can approximate the binary direct tokenisation optimisation problem with an approximation ratio better than this constant, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.∎

While the constant above (i.e.,
1.000002
) is remarkably small, we note that our proof makes no attempt to optimise this bound.
Our lemma’s main takeaway is that it is not possible to compute
D-2-TOK
with approximation ratios arbitrarily close to 1 in polynomial time.
Other larger bounds likely exist and, in fact, it might even be possible that there is no constant-factor approximation for
D-2-TOK
at all.

4.3
Binary Bottom-Up Tokenisation is Hard to Decide and Approximate

This section addresses the computational hardness of finding an optimal merge sequence in binary bottom-up tokenisation.
We establish that the problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard in
section

4.3.1
.
We then prove that the problem is also hard to approximate in
section

4.3.2
, thus showing that it is not in
\mathsf{PTAS}
, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.
As for the direct case, our argument proceeds by first proving the hardness of the
decision problem
, and then leveraging this result to demonstrate the hardness of a corresponding gap problem.

4.3.1
The Binary Bottom-Up Tokenisation Problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-Complete

As before, we use a reduction from
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
to prove this problem’s NP-hardness.

Reduction 2
.

3-OCC-MAX2SAT
decision problem and the binary alphabet

.
Again, for each variable

{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}}&\mathtt{if}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}=\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j^{\prime}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}}&\mathtt{if}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}=\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}=\neg{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j^{\prime}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}}}&\mathtt{if}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j},\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}_{j^{\prime}}\end{array}\right\}_{i=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}}

\displaystyle\times 1

These subdataset multiplicities are
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}4
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}2(2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime\prime}+3)+1=23
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}2(2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime\prime}+3)+1=115
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}2(2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}+2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime\prime}+3)+1=575
.
We set the vocabulary size to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}=10{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
and the target compressed length to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}=(8{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+1){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}+6{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}+4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime\prime}+3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}=5398{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+575+3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R2}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
to represent the
B-2-TOK
instance
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
constructed by this reduction.
As before, this is a polynomial-time
reduction.
We now prove the equivalence

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})\iff{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\uparrow}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R2}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
which shows the reduction’s correctness and thus that
B-2-TOK
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

Theorem 3
.

The binary bottom-up tokenisation decision problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

Proof sketch.

This proof is done in two steps.

Forward step (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\uparrow}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
).

See full proof in
lemma

3
in
appendix

D
.
Assume the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance admits an assignment

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}

satisfying at least
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
clauses.
We construct a merge sequence

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}={\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{1}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{2}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{3}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{4}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{5}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{6}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{4},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}_{6}
are

that appear in every valid tokeniser solution and ensure that all strings corresponding to single variables are properly compressed;
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\circledcirc}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\circledcirc}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}}
otherwise.
Applying
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}
to the string subdatasets

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
gives the fixed compressed length
5398{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+575
.
For the strings
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{5}
, the construction ensures that each clause compresses to 2 tokens if at least one of its two literals is true under
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
, and remains at 3 tokens otherwise.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
satisfies at least
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
clauses, we obtain at most
3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
symbols.
Compression thus satisfies the budget
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
.

Backward step (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\uparrow}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R2}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
).

See full proof in
lemma

4
in
appendix

E
.
We consider
sat
-compliant
direct
tokenisers, which must contain all tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}
and must
contain either
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
for each
