.
Again, an optimal direct tokeniser for the
B-2-TOK
-instance is always
sat
-compliant, which is enforced by datasets
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
.
We then show that if such a tokeniser achieves the desired compression, it must correspond to an assignment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
which satisfies the desired number of clauses.
To finish, we show that,
for any instance generated by
2
,
a
sat
-compliant direct tokeniser always corresponds to a bottom-up tokeniser with the same compression quality.
∎

4.3.2
The Binary Bottom-Up Tokenisation Gap Problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard

As in
section

4.2.2
, we perform a reduction from a gap variant to show hardness of approximation.

Theorem 4
.

The binary bottom-up tokenisation gap problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.
Thus, the binary bottom-up tokenisation optimisation problem is not in
\mathsf{PTAS}
, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.

Proof sketch.

See proof in
appendix

F
.
A similar proof to
Theorem

2
applies here, except with different values.
We find that no polynomial-time algorithm can solve the binary bottom-up tokenisation optimisation problem with an approximation ratio better than
1.0000001
, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.∎

5
Unary Tokenisation is Hard to Decide

We now move on to the unary tokenisation case.
Here, we work with alphabets composed of a single symbol, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}a}\}
.
As
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}a}^{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}\}
, it follows that unary character-strings
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
may only differ from one another in their length.
There exists thus an isomorphism (given by the function
|\cdot|
and its inverse) between these character-strings and their
string-lengths
,
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}
.
A natural notation for such problems is then
to work directly with string-lengths.
In this section, we will thus represent
a character-string
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{*}
by its length
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}
;
a dataset
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M}

by the lengths of its strings
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}_{m}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}_{m}}
;
and a vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}^{+}
by a set of string-lengths
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}\subset{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}_{+}
.
A subword-string is then a sequence of such string-lengths,
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}

and we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{detok}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{sum}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}})\hskip 22.99988pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}]({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}\argmin_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}|,\text{s.t.,}\,{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}={\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{sum}}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}})

(11)

where we overload the functions
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{detok}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}
to handle this unary-strings representation.
Note that all these definitions are equivalent (up to an isomorphism) to the definitions in
section

3
.

When posing either the optimisation or decision version of the unary tokenisation problems, we could thus work with either representation of our data (as strings or string-lengths) and the solutions must be the same.
However, the complexity of an algorithm is typically measured as a function of the length of its input.
If this input is a unary string, the input will be as long as this string’s length.
If this input is a number, however, this input’s length behaves logarithmically on the value of the number itself (as this number would typically be encoded in a compact binary representation).
When dealing with problems such as unary tokenisation, this introduces an important subtlety: the problem’s complexity status may change depending on how we represent it (with strings or string-lengths).
If such a problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard when either representation is given, it is called
strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
.
If this problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard only in its string-length representation, but not when represented using unary strings, it is
weakly
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
.
7
7

7

Note that the opposite case—where a problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard only when representing the data as strings, but not strings-lengths—is not possible, as strings have a larger size than their lengths.

Importantly,
1

applies only to strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-hard unary problems; as the trivial identity we use in its proof would not be valid for unary problems with string-length representations.
For unary tokenisation, the string representation (where strings are explicitly represented) is more natural, and we are thus interested in strong
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness.

5.1
Unary Direct Tokenisation is Strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-Complete

In this section, we prove that the unary direct tokenisation problem is strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
In
appendix

G
, we prove that the problem is in
\mathsf{NP}
, even if the input is in string-length representation.
To prove
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of unary direct tokenisation, we then design a polynomial-time reduction from the well-known vertex cover problem (
vertex-cover
).
8
8

8

The
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
vertex-cover
was proven by
9
in his groundbreaking paper introducing the very concept of
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness, and can be found in the textbook by
6
.

Let
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}})
represent a finite, simple, undirected graph with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}^{\prime})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}^{\prime}\}
.
A set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}
is a
vertex cover
if, for every edge
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}}
, we have that either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}^{\prime}
is in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}
.
Given a budget
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}
, the vertex cover problem requires deciding whether a graph has a vertex cover of at most
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}
vertices.

Definition 3
.

Given a graph
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}})
and a budget
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}
, the
vertex cover decision problem
asks whether there exists a vertex cover
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}
with
|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}|\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}
in this graph.

For convenience, we will write
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{VC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})
for a function which returns
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}
if its input is a satisfiable instance of the
vertex-cover
decision problem, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}}
otherwise.
We now provide a polynomial-time reduction from
vertex-cover
to
D-1-TOK
, which will prove
D-1-TOK
’s
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness.

Reduction 3
.

Consider an instance
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})
of
vertex-cover
and
let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}+1)^{4}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}=|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}|
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}=|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}}|
.
Now, let
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{enc}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\big)=j+j^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}+j^{3}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}^{2}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}B}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}^{4}
.
We construct three subdatasets from this graph as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}{{}_{j}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}\}\,\cup\,\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}B}\},

\displaystyle\texttt{where }{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}{{}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{enc}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\big)

vertex-strings

(12a)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}j}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}\},

\displaystyle\texttt{where }{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}j}}={\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{enc}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\big)+{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}B}

cover-strings

(12b)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}j},{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}j^{\prime}}}}\mid({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}})\},

\displaystyle\texttt{where }{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell}^{\prime\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}j},{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}j^{\prime}}}={\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{enc}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\big)+{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{enc}}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j^{\prime}}\big)+{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}B}

edge-strings

(12c)

Finally, we merge these subdatasets to form a dataset
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
,
and set

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+1+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}=3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}+1-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}
.

As before, we complete our
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proof by showing this to be a valid reduction, i.e., that

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{VC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})\iff{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{1}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R3}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}))
.
Notably, our reduction outputs (in polynomial time, as all lengths are polynomially bounded in the size of the original instance) an instance of the unary direct tokenisation problem in string form.
As such, by proving the correctness of this reduction, we prove the
strong

\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of
D-1-TOK
.

Theorem 5
.

The unary direct tokenisation decision problem is strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

Proof sketch.

This proof is done in two steps.

Forward step (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{VC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{1}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R3}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}))
).

See full proof in
lemma

6
in
appendix

H
.
Suppose that the given instance of
vertex-cover
is true, i.e., that
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{VC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}
.
Now, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}^{\star}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}

be a vertex cover which satisfies this instance.
Then we can build a tokeniser with vocabulary:

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\ell}{{}_{j}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}B}}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}a}^{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\ell}^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}j}}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v}_{j}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}^{\star}}\}
.
This tokeniser will encode: all strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
as a single symbol;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}
strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
with a single symbol and others with 2; and
all strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
with two symbols (as, per our assumption, all edges have at least one vertex in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}}^{\star}}
).
This means the total amount of tokens used is:

({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+1)+(2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})+2{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}
.

Backward step (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{1}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R3}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi}))\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{VC}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{V}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{E}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\psi})
).

See full proof in
lemma

7
in
appendix

I
.
We prove this lemma in 4 steps.
First, we show that all string-lengths in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}
are unique.
Second, we show that an optimal tokeniser’s vocabulary must contain only full strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}
.
Third, we show that an optimal tokeniser’s vocabulary must include all strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
.
Fourth, we show that if a compression of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}
is achieved, than the corresponding
vertex-cover
instance must be true.
Notably, three of these steps rely on the fact that we can use
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}
as a numerical base to prove the uniqueness of both: (i) individual string-lengths, as well as (ii) their pairwise summed values.∎

Interestingly,
the unary direct tokenisation problem is tightly related to the problem of choosing denominations for a coin system.
In fact, the application of the function
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{N}}}}}]({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\ell})
is equivalent to the change-making problem; a problem shown to be (weakly)
\mathsf{NP}
-hard by
13
and a classic example for dynamic programming.
(Note that this problem is only weakly
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, as we can solve it in polynomial time when the input is given in string form.)
The unary direct tokenisation problem can thus be equivalently seen as a
general optimal denomination problem
, where—given a set of common currency transactions—one must select optimal coin denominations for a currency; see
17
for a discussion of this problem.

Corollary 1
.

The general optimal denomination decision problem is strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

5.2
A Variant of Unary Bottom-up Tokenisation is (at Least) Weakly
\mathsf{NP}
-Hard

While direct tokenisation over a unary alphabet is strongly
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, our current picture of the complexity of its bottom-up counterpart is more nuanced.
In bottom-up tokenisation, one must find a merge sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}
which is then applied (by
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\uparrow}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}]({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})
)
exhaustively
and
in sequence
, replacing all occurrences of each pair one at a time.
A variant of this problem—termed
optimal pair encoding (OPE) tokenisation
—relaxes this requirement, using the merge sequence for a different purpose: to define a
merge-extracted vocabulary

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}m}=({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}s}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}})\}

The final tokenisation is then produced by optimally applying this vocabulary, which can be done using the direct encoding function (
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}}]({\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})
).
This approach thus ensures that a merge is used only if it contributes to the most efficient segmentation overall.
Notably, this variation was used by
15
and formally analysed by
10
.
Now, let the
unary OPE tokenisation problem
be defined similarly to the other
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}n}
-ary tokenisation problems (in
1
),
but while constraining the search space to the set of OPE tokenisers:

\mathcal{T}_{\mathtt{ope}}\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\texttt{def}}}{{=}}}\{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}}]\mid{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{m}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{M}}^{*}\}
.
Having defined the decision problem, we now establish its computational hardness.

Theorem 6
.

The unary optimal pair encoding decision problem is (at least) weakly
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.

Proof sketch.

The full proof can be found in
appendix

K
.
Inclusion in
\mathsf{NP}
follows from
10
. The proof of
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness is achieved via a polynomial-time reduction from the addition chain sequence decision problem (see
appendix

J
for a formal definition), which is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-complete when its input numbers are encoded in binary
(
4
)
.
The reduction reveals a natural connection between the two problems: finding the shortest addition chain for a set of numbers is equivalent to a special case of unary optimal pair encoding where every string in the dataset must be compressed into a single token.
∎

6
Conclusion and Limitations

We provided several hardness results on bottom-up and direct tokenisation with
bounded alphabets, thus answering open questions posed by both
10
and
19
.
A number of open questions remain, however, in particular with respect to approximability.
For instance, while we showed that the binary tokenisation optimisation problems
cannot be approximated arbitrarily well (unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
)—and while it seems likely that the
lower bound provided in the proof of
theorem

2

can be significantly lifted—it is unclear whether any constant approximation ratio can even be obtained.
With respect to decision problems, while we showed strong
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of unary direct tokenisation, we were so far only able to prove: (i) weak
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness of unary OPE tokenisation, and (ii) no hardness result for unary (standard) bottom-up tokenisation.
Finally, the results of our work are limited in that we consider (i) compression as objective, and (ii) bottom-up and direct tokenisation
only; the hardness of both other objectives and variants remains open.
Overall, however, our results show that tokenisation remains a hard problem, even when restricted to small (even binary or unary) alphabets.
Future work should thus explore provably good approximation algorithms.

Appendix A
Proof of Forward Step of
Theorem

1

Lemma 1
.

If a
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance is satisfiable, then the
D-2-TOK
instance output by
1
is also satisfiable. Formally:

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
.

Proof.

To prove this forward step of
Theorem

1
, we first establish that a satisfiable
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance guarantees the existence of a binary direct tokeniser that meets the compression target.
Assume a
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
instance of the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
problem is satisfiable, i.e., that
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
is true.
We must prove that, in this case,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
is also true.
Now, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}
be any satisfying solution to the
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
instance.
We will denote the number of clauses satisfied by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}
, noting that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}
by assumption.
We can construct a tokeniser from this solution as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}\bigcup\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\Big\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}\hskip 11.49994pt\bigcup\hskip 11.49994pt\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\texttt{ if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\texttt{ else }{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\Big\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}

(13)

Note that—as required by our reduction—this tokeniser has vocabulary size
|{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\Sigma}|+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}
, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K}=5{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
tokens were added.
Under this tokeniser, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1})=\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}\rangle},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}\rangle},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}\hskip 11.49994pt(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 1}})

\displaystyle\mid 1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\Big\}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}

(14a)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2})=\bigg\{\begin{array}[]{llr}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 3}})&\texttt{ if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 3}})&\texttt{ else}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mid 1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3})=\bigg\{\begin{array}[]{llr}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 2}})&\hskip 22.99988pt\texttt{ if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}^{\star}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 2}})&\texttt{ else}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mid 1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4})=\Bigg\{\begin{array}[]{llr}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 2}})&\texttt{ if }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 2}})&\texttt{ elif }{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1},\,{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}&(\text{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}length 3}})&\texttt{ else}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mid 1\leq i\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\times 1

where we override function
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}]
to apply elementwise to a full dataset of character-strings, instead of to a unique
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}
.
Consequently, we get the compressed lengths:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1})=4\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}=252\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\hskip 22.99988pt

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2})=3\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}=63\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J},

(15a)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3})=2\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}=14\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J},\hskip 22.99988pt

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4})=3\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}

(15b)

We have that each character-string in dataset
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
is compressed to 2 symbols if either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{1}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}_{i}^{2}
are true, and otherwise is kept at 3 symbols; the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}
satisfied clauses in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}^{\star}}
will thus be compressed to 2 symbols and the unsatisfied clauses to 3.
Summing these values together, we get the compressed length of the entire dataset under this tokeniser:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})=329{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}
.
Finally:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}\implies 329{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{\star}}\leq 329{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+3\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}

(16)

This completes this proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Backward Step of
Theorem

1

Before starting our lemma’s proof, we define a few notions which will be useful throughout it.
First, we define a
sat
-compliant
tokeniser to be any tokeniser which:
(i) contains all tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
; and
(ii) contains either
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
for each
j\in\{1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}\}
.
Otherwise, we call the tokeniser
sat
-noncompliant
.
Given the vocabulary of a
sat
-compliant tokeniser, we can easily build an assignment to a
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance with the following function:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}})=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}},\texttt{ where }\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{T}}&\texttt{ if }{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathtt{F}}&\texttt{ elif }{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}\end{array}\right.

Further, we will define as a
101-string
any character-string of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}
, and as a
10101-string
any character-string of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{+}{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}1}
.
(The
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{+}
notation stands for a sequence of one or more
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}
characters.)
Considering the datasets output by
1
, we know that
there are no 101-strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
.
Further, we know that each unique 101-string appears in datasets
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
exactly
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}
times, respectively, and exactly 3 times in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
. (This is due to us working with the three-occurrences variant of
MAX2SAT
and to the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{2j-1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}={\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}0}^{2j}
.)
We now prove the following lemma.

Lemma 2
.

If the
D-2-TOK
instance output by
1
is satisfiable, then the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance which generated it is as well. Formally:

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
.

Proof.

Assume this
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
instance of
D-2-TOK
—where
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})={\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
—is satisfiable, i.e., that
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))
evaluates to true.
We must prove that, in this case,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
also evaluates to true.
Now, let
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}
be an arbitrary optimal solution to
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta})
.
We know, by definition, that:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{Tok}_{\text{\pointer}}^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{R1}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma}))\iff\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\Big)

(19)

We can thus prove this lemma by showing the following implication:

\displaystyle\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\Big)\implies{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathrm{3OM2S}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})

(20)

We will proceed in four steps:

1

we prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}
must include all tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
;

2

we prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}
must, in addition to the tokens above, only include tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
;

3

we prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}
may only include, for each
j
, either token
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
;

4

finally, we prove that, if
\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}\Big)
, we can build a variable assignment which satisfies this
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{C}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma})
.

Note that, together, steps
1
to
3
show that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}
must be the vocabulary of a
sat
-compliant tokeniser; in step
4
, we will then rely on the function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}
(defined above in
appendix

B
) to convert this vocabulary into a satisfying assignment
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathpzc{x}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g}({\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}})
of the
3-OCC-MAX2SAT
instance.
∎

LemmaProofStep 1
.

(Step
1
).

An optimal tokeniser must include all tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\Big\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}

(21)

Proof.

We prove this step by contradiction.
Assume there exists an optimal tokeniser with vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
which does not include
t>0
of the tokens above.
Now, choose an arbitrary set of
t
tokens in this vocabulary which are not of the above form, and replace them with the missing tokens in this set.
We denote this new tokeniser’s vocabulary by
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
.
Note that the strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
with these missing tokens were represented with at least
2
symbols under
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
, but with a single token under
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1})\geq(4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+t){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\hskip 22.99988pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1})=4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}

(22)

Further, note that under
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
, we have that strings in dataset
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
are compressed to at most two symbols, while strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
are compressed to at most three symbols:

\displaystyle\forall{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\leq 2,\hskip 11.49994pt\forall{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.3281,0.4961,0.9375}c}}})\leq 3

(23)

To improve on this compressed length,
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
must, thus, compress strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
to a single symbol, or strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
to one or two symbols.
Notably, this can only be done if the non-compliant tokens in
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
contain 101-strings.
This is because, to compress a string in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
to a single symbol, the full character-string must become a token, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
only includes 101-strings.
Moreover, under
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
, strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
are already compressed to at most
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\langle{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\rangle}
. To further compress them, tokeniser
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
must include tokens which cross the “middle” of this character-string, which would make this tokens at least have a 101 prefix or suffix. We consider the best case scenario, which is if they are exactly 101-strings, as any longer string will be at most as frequent as it.

As discussed above, however, each 101-string appears at most:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}
times in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}
times in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
, and

3
times in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
.
This gives us a best case scenario—in which all the strings in which a new token appears are compressed to a single symbol—where:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4})\leq t({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}+2({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3))

(24)

As the difference in
eq.

22
is of at least
t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}
tokens, we put these together:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\leq t({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}+2({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3))-t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}

(25)

As
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}>{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}+2({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3)
,
this difference is smaller than zero, implying that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
improves on
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
. This shows a contradiction, which completes our proof.
∎

LemmaProofStep 2
.

(Step
2
).

An optimal tokeniser must include all tokens of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}

\displaystyle\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\Big\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}\hskip 11.49994pt\texttt{and}\hskip 11.49994pt{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\mathtt{opt}}}\subset\Big\{{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}\Big\}_{j=1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}}

(26)

Proof.

As before, we prove this step by contradiction.
Given step
1
, we know an optimal tokeniser includes all tokens
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
.
Now, assume there exists an optimal tokeniser with vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
with
t>0
tokens which are not of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
; note that these
t
tokens are
sat
-noncompliant.
Choose an arbitrary set of
t
unused compliant tokens—i.e., with form
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{T}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1},{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathbf{x}^{\mathtt{F}}_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}1}
—to replace the non-compliant tokens with, forming a new tokeniser’s vocabulary
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
.
Both these vocabularies compress strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1}
equally:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1})=4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f},\hskip 22.99988pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{1})=4{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}

(27)

For strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
: if the entire string is in the vocabulary, it is encoded as a single token; otherwise, it is represented with two symbols.
Under
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
, there are
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}
tokens covering strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
.
Under
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
, there are only
({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}-t)
tokens covering strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2}
.
This implies:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2})=(3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}+t){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime},\hskip 22.99988pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{2})=3{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}

(28)

Finally, for strings in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4}
, a similar argument to the previous step applies:
(i) only tokens containing 101-strings can compress these datasets;
(ii) each 101-string appears at most
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3
times in them;
(iii) each 101-string will lead to at most two symbols being saved.
As
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
differs from
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
in
t
tokens, we get that it will improve on it by at most:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{3}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}}_{4})\leq 2t({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3)

(29)

Summing together the compression on all datasets, we get that their difference is bounded by:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathfrak{G}}_{\ell}}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{tok}}_{\text{\pointer}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}],{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{D}})\leq 2t({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3)-t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}

(30)

As
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime}>2({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}^{\prime\prime}+3)
, this difference is smaller than zero, implying that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}
improves on
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}
. This shows a contradiction, which completes our proof.
∎

LemmaProofStep 3

).

{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✗}}}

variables, and thus neither of those two for
t>0
other variables.

token of all non-assigned variables.
Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}{\color[rgb]{0.8633,0.1484,0.4961}\mathcal{S}}_{\text{✓}}}

-compliant.
